### [A Generative AI-powered navigation system for precision CBCT-guided lung biopsies: a randomized controlled trial](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03355-1)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `64` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03355-1`

Lung puncture biopsy is the gold standard for the qualitative diagnosis of lung nodules. Cone Beam CT (CBCT) is commonly used for guiding punctures to locate lung nodules. However, the poor quality of CBCT images and the limited intelligence of surgical guidance systems have lowered the efficiency of puncture procedures, particularly for small and complex lesions. This study developed and validated a robust and accurate Generative AI-Powered Puncture Surgery Navigation System for lung punctures (GPS-Lung), demonstrating clinical applicability through a multicenter randomized controlled trial (Registration number: NCT06762613). The clinical results showed significant improvements in surgeon performance with the assistance of the integrated GPS-Lung system. Specifically, the system facilitated a more standardized and efficient operational process, reducing number of punctures (2.0 ± 1.2 vs. 2.5 ± 1.7, P = 0.005), shortening operative time (20.4 ± 5.6 min vs. 23.2 ± 6.3 min, P = 0.001), decreasing radiation exposure (4357.4 ± 3427.3 μGy·m2 vs. 5570.9 ± 4846.7 μGy·m2, P = 0.033), and reducing pneumothorax complications (12.7% vs. 23.9%, P = 0.033). Taken together, the proposed system demonstrates promising clinical and translational value in lung nodule puncture intervention. (Clinical Trials Registration: NCT06762613). Clinical Trials Registration: NCT06762613, Registration Date: March 26, 2025. (https://clinicaltrials.gov/study/NCT06762613?term=NCT06762613&amp;rank=1&amp;tab=history).

### [A Typology of Moderator Practices and Community Guidelines in Online Mental Health Communities to Establish a Framework for Digital Safety: Systematized Review](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e99367)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `59` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e99367`

Background: The rise of online mental health communities has reshaped the ways individuals seek and receive mental health support. Although online mental health communities (OMHCs) offer a valuable space for peer support, including through experience-sharing, they also present challenges, including moderation practices to cultivate supportive interactions and secure member safety. No comprehensive framework of moderation practices has been established. Developing such an understanding is important, as moderators play a central role in facilitating a sense of community and determining whether OMHCs function as safe and supportive spaces. Objective: This study aimed to develop typologies of moderator practices used to support safety and facilitate a sense of community within OMHCs for people with mental health problems, and the content of OMHC community guidelines. Methods: A systematized review of empirical research studies focusing on moderation practices and community guidelines in OMHCs was conducted using MEDLINE, Web of Science, Association for Computing Machinery Digital Library, supplemented with a purposive search to identify gray literature on community guidelines for OMHC moderation. Inclusion criteria: online, text-based component, asynchronous or synchronous moderation, mental health–related focus, and human moderation. Exclusion criteria: crisis-focused, audience-based, nonmoderated or bot-moderated, profession not peer support, non-English, and academic review or thesis. The search period was 2014 to 2024. A bespoke data extraction sheet was piloted, then populated, capturing document, OMHC, member, and moderator characteristics. Thematic analysis was used to synthesize the findings relating to each objective. Results: The systematized search identified 11,347 studies and other documents, from which 80 studies were included, comprising 48 empirical papers and 32 gray literature documents. These related to 52 different OMHCs. Four categories of moderator practices were identified: (1) premoderation and postmoderation processes, (2) reactive moderation processes, (3) building the community, and (4) monitoring members. Premoderation and postmoderation processes were used within 41 (79%) of the 52 OMHCs. Three categories of community guideline content were identified: (1) content of post, (2) member conduct, and (3) moderation. The category, content of post, was the most widely used component of community guidelines, used in 35 (67%) of the 52 OMHCs. Conclusions: The two typologies provide a foundational framework that can be adapted and applied to community guidelines and moderation strategies across a range of contexts. When operationalized, they could provide benefit to people with mental health problems by using them to guide the development of moderator training, digital tools, and governance policies that align with the specific characteristics and objectives of individual OMHCs. Effective translation and application of these typologies require careful, nuanced consideration of each community’s goals, membership, and cultural context to ensure their relevance, effectiveness, and impact. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/982fd77129c3800de3a2629c01e03c45" />

Abbott said Wednesday it plans to spin out its nutrition business into an independent, publicly traded company. The business has approximately $8.5 billion in annual sales and includes brands like Pedialyte, Similac and Ensure. In Abbott’s third quarter, nutrition brought in revenue of $2.26 billion, representing year-over-year growth of about 5%. Abbott’s nutrition business has been at the center of lawsuits as well as a federal investigation into the manufacturing and handling of a recall of powdered infant formula in 2022 due to potential contamination. In September, the company announced a $385 million settlement with the Department of Justice to resolve claims as well as lawsuits filed by certain relators and the attorneys general in several states.

### [Capacity, Collaboration, and Thematic Structure in Health AI Research Over the Past Decade in the Gulf Cooperation Council: Scientometric Analysis](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e105709)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `61` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e105709`

Background: Health AI investment is accelerating across the Gulf Cooperation Council (GCC), yet the region’s health AI research output has never been systematically quantified. National research capacity is a structural precondition for the safe local development and governance of clinical AI, but no scientometric baseline exists for the 6 GCC states. Objective: We characterized the volume, growth, collaboration structure, and thematic composition of GCC health AI research and examined whether national output aligns with an existing measure of informatics readiness. Methods: We conducted an ecological scientometric analysis of all health AI publications affiliated with the 6 GCC states (Bahrain, Kuwait, Oman, Qatar, Saudi Arabia, and the United Arab Emirates) for 2011 to 2024 (the last complete indexing year at extraction) using the open bibliographic database OpenAlex. A publication was eligible if it included both an AI family and a health or medicine family concept above a 0.30 relevance threshold. Bibliometric indicators (output, compound annual growth, per capita intensity, citation impact, international and intraregional collaboration, and institutional concentration) were computed; coauthorship and concept co-occurrence networks were constructed and clustered by modularity. National health AI intensity was correlated (Spearman) with the Health Informatics Readiness Index (HIRI) and Global Digital Health Monitor (GDHM) phase. Results: Of 112,480 GCC-affiliated AI publications, 3512 met the health AI definition. Regional output rose from 18 (2011) to 904 (2024) publications per year (compound annual growth=35.2%), with 86.4% (3034/3512) published from 2020 onward and a sharp inflection from 2019 to 2021. Saudi Arabia contributed 59.6% (2094/3512) of the output by itself and 81.5% (2862/3512) together with the United Arab Emirates; Qatar led on a per capita basis (approximately 136 publications per million population). International collaboration (2551/3512, 72.6% of the papers) predominated over intra-GCC collaboration (164/3327, 4.9%), with the latter being inversely related to national output. The principal external partners were India, the United States, Pakistan, the United Kingdom, and Egypt. Output spanned 3186 institutions; concentration ranged from 89.4% (328/367) of a country’s output among the top 3 institutions for Qatar to 34.4% (721/2094) for Saudi Arabia. Concept co-occurrence was classified into 4 themes—classic machine learning clinical prediction, medical imaging and oncology, COVID-19 response, and diabetic eye disease. Health AI intensity (ρ=+0.60) and, more strongly, absolute output (ρ=+0.89) correlated with the HIRI; absolute output was also correlated with GDHM phase (ρ=+0.83). Conclusions: GCC health AI research has grown rapidly but unevenly, is heavily dependent on international collaboration, is concentrated in medical imaging, and—outside Saudi Arabia—remains anchored in single national institutions. Informatics readiness was associated with both the intensity and the volume of national research output. The findings establish a reproducible baseline for monitoring regional health AI research capacity as GCC health systems move toward clinical AI deployment.

### [Conditions and Pathways for Integrating Smart Care Robots Into Long-Term Care Facilities: Qualitative Study From Nursing Assistants’ Perspectives](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e97371)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `54` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e97371`

Background: Smart care robots are increasingly viewed as a potential way to address care burden and support care quality in long-term care facilities. However, their integration into routine care practice remains limited, and the conditions required for their effective implementation are not yet well understood from the perspective of frontline nursing assistants. Objective: This study aimed to clarify nursing assistants’ core functional expectations of smart care robots in long-term care facilities and to explore the key conditions for their integration into practice. Methods: A qualitative descriptive study was conducted. Using purposive sampling, 31 frontline nursing assistants were recruited from 5 long-term care facilities in Chengdu, Sichuan Province, China, with recruitment assistance from facility administrators and eligibility screening by the research team. Semistructured face-to-face interviews were conducted between September and November 2025. Data were analyzed using framework analysis. Results: Framework analysis produced 4 analytical domains: real-world care needs, limited facilitating conditions, major anticipated barriers, and preparatory pathways for implementation. Participants expected robots to support safety monitoring and risk warning, standardized checking and management, repetitive physical tasks, and basic interaction. Although some openness to technology and organizational support existed, these facilitating conditions were insufficient for routine implementation. Key barriers included unclear human-robot collaboration models, inadequate organizational capacity, potential new care risks, and weak external support. Participants emphasized organizational assessment, contextual adaptation, pilot testing, and structured training as essential preparatory steps. Emotional support functions were also valued for alleviating caregivers’ emotional burden in high-intensity care work. Conclusions: Openness to technology, supportive organizational climates, and positive policy and social signals provided an initial foundation for considering robot integration. However, uncertainties remained around human-robot collaboration, risk management, organizational capacity, and concrete external support. Organizational assessment, contextual adaptation, real-world pilot testing, and practical training warrant further evaluation before wider implementation. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/84c7e8f97cc0f2062878f2bbdad78868" />

### [Cross-Domain Chest Radiograph Report Generation Under Dataset Shift: Bidirectional Audit Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e95888)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `68` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e95888`

Background: Cross-domain chest radiograph report generation may produce formally valid reports that are nevertheless repetitive, weakly case-specific, or missing clinically relevant findings. These failures can be missed when evaluation relies on a single language-overlap or clinical-label score. Objective: This study aimed to evaluate cross-domain report generation with a bidirectional layered audit, characterize direction-specific and end point–specific failure profiles under a composite training-side control and 3 decoding policies, and explore whether selected audit dimensions remained informative for a contemporary medical vision language model. Methods: A fixed R2Gen-style system was evaluated bidirectionally on 575 IU X-ray and 401 MIMIC-CXR (Medical Information Mart for Intensive Care Chest X-Ray) target records. The audit included surface validity, complete-report recurrence and concentration, a predefined prefix end point, lexical diversity, official Stanford CheXpert/NegBio labels, paired cluster-bootstrap inference, and blinded review by 2 medical trainees. The primary human audit included 210 reports from 70 randomly sampled cases, with blinded adjudication of unresolved primary end point disagreements. A 60-report targeted panel was used for failure-mode analysis. Separately, a fixed MedGemma 1.5 4B configuration generated reports for all 976 records. Results: In MIMIC-CXR–to–IU X-ray transfer, out of 575 reports, ON (protocol-defined training-side control enabled)+B4 (beam search with a beam size of 4) reduced the predefined recurrent prefix from 575 (100%) to 8 (1.4%; Holm-adjusted =.002). However, 569 (99.0%) ON+B4 outputs still belonged to exact duplicate groups. In IU X-ray–to–MIMIC-CXR transfer, the 14-category CheXpert/NegBio micro- was higher for ON+B4 than for OFF (protocol-defined training-side control disabled)+B4 (0.1687 vs 0.0744; Holm-adjusted =.01). This apparent advantage was not broad-based: after cardiomegaly was excluded in a post hoc descriptive sensitivity analysis, the difference changed from +0.0944 to −0.0610. Text-level tracing showed that the original contrast was mainly driven by a labeler response to recurrent, semantically normal heart-size wording. Across the primary human-audit cells, major omissions occurred in 12 to 15 out of 35 reports, and major unsupported findings occurred in 10 to 16 of 35 reports, with no consistent configuration ordering observed. The exploratory MedGemma micro- was 0.4722 on IU X-ray and 0.4340 on MIMIC-CXR, but 381 out of 575 (66.3%) IU outputs belonged to exact duplicate groups. Conclusions: No configuration performed consistently well across end points, and no single metric captured all forms of cross-domain degradation. The MedGemma stress test suggested that recurrence and label-traceability checks remain useful across architectures, although it was not a matched performance comparison. Cross-domain report generators should undergo bidirectional evaluation, final-text verification, and image-grounded human review before external validation or clinical deployment.

### [Environmental Impact of Digital Health Care Technologies in Reducing Carbon Footprints: Scoping Review](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e88341)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `56` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e88341`

Background: Globally, the carbon footprint of the health care sector is significant and growing, and digital health care technologies (DHTs) offer unique opportunities to reduce the carbon footprint of health and care systems. However, their environmental impact has not been adequately investigated. Objective: This scoping review aims to explore the environmental impact of DHTs, focusing on reducing their carbon footprint and evaluating methods for assessing that impact. Methods: This scoping review followed the method outlined by Arksey and O’Malley, adhering to the PRISMA-ScR (Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses extension for Scoping Reviews) guidelines. The databases of MEDLINE, Scopus, and Web of Science were searched for studies published in English with no time limitation. Search terms were categorized under three broader concepts: “Digital Health Technologies,” “Health and Care,” and “Carbon Footprint.” Studies were included based on screening titles and abstracts, followed by review of full-text papers by at least 2 reviewers. Narrative synthesis was used to report the findings. Results: The database search yielded 4274 studies. Following screening and reference list checks, 70 studies were selected for inclusion. The earliest included study was published in 2007, with the majority originating from the United States (n=26) and the United Kingdom (n=12). Most studies assessed telemedicine or telehealth interventions. Retrospective analyses predominated, although prospective, cross-sectional, mixed method, and life cycle assessment (LCA) designs were also reported. Environmental impact was most often evaluated through travel-related carbon savings, with some studies incorporating energy use, medical equipment or resources, infrastructure, or waste. LCA studies applied process-based approaches that included detailed activity- and product-specific data, as well as Environmentally Extended Input-Output (EEIO) models. In most cases, carbon footprint measurements were based on assumptions, with calculations relying on published emission factors or calculators. Some studies have expressed CO emissions using emission equivalencies, such as equivalent carbon sequestration and equivalent CO emissions. Overall, DHTs demonstrated positive environmental impacts by reducing carbon footprint. However, negative impacts, especially electricity consumption and the lifecycle impacts of digital devices, were also noted. Conclusions: DHTs are associated with reductions in carbon emissions and related environmental benefits, with telemedicine showing potential to lower the carbon footprint of the health care sector. However, evidence highlights the negative impacts associated with energy demand and the full lifecycle of digital devices. Given the substantial heterogeneity in assessment methods and outcome measures across studies, these findings should be interpreted with caution. To capture the true environmental implications of digital health, more rigorous, standardized, and comprehensive assessment methods are required. Bridging these methodological gaps is essential to ensure that DHTs not only support sustainable health care delivery but also make a meaningful contribution to mitigating global climate change. Trial Registration: OSF Registries osf.io/gdnve; https://osf.io/gdnve/overview <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/cedbc54ab8fca1967d787058dca84e51" />

### [Global and regional burden of measles and vaccine coverage among children under 5 years, 1990−2023: a Global Burden of Disease Study](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04661-7)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `68` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04661-7`

Measles remains a leading cause of vaccine-preventable burden among children, particularly in low-income settings. Despite decades of progress, there are indications of measles resurgence due to stagnation in vaccine coverage. Here, using the Global Burden of Diseases, Injuries and Risk Factors Study (GBD) 2023, we systematically assessed measles incidence, mortality, disability-adjusted life years (DALYs) and vaccine coverage (measles-containing vaccine 1 (MCV1) and measles-containing vaccine 2 (MCV2)) among children under 5 years of age across 204 countries and territories from 1990 to 2023. Between 1990 and 2019, global measles incidence and mortality declined by approximately 80% and 90%, respectively, but rebounded sharply after 2020, with 15.6 million cases and 118,000 deaths estimated in 2023 (+40% and +12% increases, respectively, between 2019 and 2023). Sub-Saharan Africa and South Asia continue to bear the highest burden. Global MCV1 coverage rose from 67.2% in 1990 to 83.6% in 2019 but stalled and declined slightly to 81.0% by 2023; MCV2 coverage increased from 14.4% in 1990 to 68.4% in 2019 and reached 71.4% by 2023. Several regions remain well below the Immunization Agenda 2030 (IA2030) target of 90% coverage. The recent measles resurgence underscores the fragility of global immunization progress and the urgent need to restore routine vaccination, close immunity gaps and strengthen outbreak preparedness. Updates to the Global Burden of Disease Study showed that although global measles incidence and mortality had declined in 1990−2019, its resurgence since 2020 reflects stalled vaccination progress worldwide.

### [Making Genomic Data Actionable in Electronic Health Records: Practical Viewpoint on Standards, Governance, and Implementation](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e99130)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `51` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e99130`

Electronic health records (EHRs) are central to patient-centered care. However, many current EHR systems do not represent, exchange, or reuse genomic findings in structured, interoperable, and clinically actionable forms. Instead, genomic results are often retained in static reports. This limits their use in routine clinical care and clinical decision support. It also restricts their future reinterpretation as scientific knowledge evolves, as well as their reuse for research within appropriate governance frameworks. To support precision and personalized medicine, we advocate that clinically relevant genomic findings should be represented within EHRs as structured, phenotype-linked, interoperable, and versioned data. Raw and intermediate genomic files, however, should remain in appropriately governed laboratory or genomics repositories. This viewpoint is intended for health policymakers, health informatics leaders, EHR developers, research and clinical genomics laboratories, and clinicians involved in implementing genomics into routine care. It is informed by selected published literature and relevant technical standards. It is also informed by operational experience at the Cyprus Institute of Neurology and Genetics and multidisciplinary discussions from the Gen2EHR Implementation Guidelines Workshop conducted within the Extended EHR@EU Data Space and European Health Data Space for Secondary Use @CY initiatives. We examine technical, organizational, clinical, ethical, legal, and governance challenges, including inconsistent reporting formats and unclear clinical responsibility. We propose a staged, workshop-informed implementation pathway for evaluation within the European Health Data Space. The pathway combines Health Level 7 Fast Health Interoperability Resources Genomics Reporting with complementary standards and terminologies. It also includes transitional dual PDF and structured Fast Health Interoperability Resources reporting, governance for access, version control, reinterpretation, and clinical responsibility, as well as multidisciplinary pilot validation in real diagnostic workflows. The clinical effectiveness and transferability of the proposed pathway across European health systems remain to be established.

### [Online Behavioral Digital Phenotyping for Depression: Scoping Review of Behavioral Features and Exploratory Assessment of Research Domain Criteria Construct Relevance](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e101329)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `61` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e101329`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; Depression is heterogeneous and dynamic, and changes in observable behavior may accompany some of its clinical manifestations. Digital devices offer new opportunities to capture this behavior in naturalistic settings continuously. However, the specific digital features captured through device interactions and their relationships to depression-related outcomes have yet to be synthesized, and their potential relevance to functional constructs is unclear. The research domain criteria (RDoC) may provide a conceptual framework for exploring potential links between digital behaviors and functional constructs. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; This scoping review aimed (1) to identify and summarize key online behavioral modalities and their specific features for depression monitoring and assessment, (2) to summarize existing evidence on associations between specific behavioral features and depression-related outcomes, and (3) to explore whether the behavioral content captured by digital measures has the potential conceptual links to RDoC constructs. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; We conducted a scoping review of 5 databases (PubMed, Scopus, Web of Science, Embase, and PsycINFO) from inception through January 2026, supplemented by citation tracking and gray literature searches. Included studies focused on online behavioral digital phenotyping for depression and reported specific behavioral features. Two reviewers (QR and JC) independently screened studies and extracted data, with disagreements resolved through discussion or consultation with a third reviewer (YH). Findings were synthesized descriptively. Two reviewers (QR and Kaili Tang) independently assessed potential conceptual links between each behavioral modality and RDoC constructs using the official RDoC construct definitions. &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; In total, 74 studies were included. Five primary behavioral modalities were identified: call behavior (n=31), SMS (n=21), screen use (n=41), app use (n=26), and keystroke dynamics (n=14), comprising 36 core features. A total of 31 (41.9%) studies reported findings attributable to individual features, but no single feature showed a consistently directed association with depression-related outcomes across studies. An exploratory RDoC assessment suggested that candidate construct relevance primarily involved social processes, sensorimotor systems, cognitive systems, and arousal and regulatory systems. &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; Online behavioral digital phenotyping captures a range of observable behaviors, but current evidence does not support interpreting individual digital behavioral features as stable markers of depression or as direct proxies for specific RDoC constructs. Future research should improve the transparency of feature definitions and reporting, strengthen the reporting of feature-level findings, and use independent validation and construct-informed study designs to enhance interpretability. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/a6007bf825b8550669a961d18d9383c5" />

### [Safety and Performance Evaluation of a Clinical Decision Support System for Imatinib Therapeutic Drug Monitoring: In Silico Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e93304)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `47` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e93304`

Background: Therapeutic drug monitoring (TDM) aims to optimize clinical outcomes but is hindered by logistical delays and susceptibility to data errors. Integrating model-informed precision dosing with point-of-care testing, fortified by rule-based validation and alerts, forms a transformative clinical decision support system (CDSS) essential for securing this complex workflow. Objective: This study evaluates, , the effectiveness of a rule-based CDSS for imatinib in detecting data-quality issues and optimizing therapeutic dosing, compared with a human TDM expert. Methods: We simulated a set of 60 virtual patients using a previously developed population pharmacokinetic model and deliberately enriched it with data-quality errors (eg, timing inaccuracies and reporting errors) to stress-test system resilience. We compared CDSS and TDM expert performances based on their error-detection capabilities and the clinical impact on therapeutic target attainment and safety. Results: The CDSS and the expert demonstrated comparable error-detection capabilities. The CDSS showed numerically higher sensitivity (53%, 95% CI 35%‐70% vs 45%, 95% CI 30%‐61%) and lower specificity (83%, 95% CI 63%‐95% vs 100%, 95% CI 81%‐100%). Clinically, the CDSS demonstrated significantly higher rates of target attainment than the expert (33/60, 55% vs 22/60, 37%; =.046). However, the CDSS resulted in 5 detrimental outcomes against 1 for the TDM expert, in which patients initially within the target range fell outside it. Conclusions: The CDSS demonstrated high efficacy in optimizing imatinib exposure while serving as a robust digital gatekeeper. While active optimization drives higher efficacy, a few observed detrimental cases highlight that algorithmic safety remains contingent on data integrity. This proof-of-concept study validates the CDSS architecture as a prerequisite for future prospective clinical trials.

### [Synthetic Medical Document Generation Pipeline (MedDocSynth) for Optical Character Recognition: Development and Validation Study on Korean Clinical Documents](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e91313)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `61` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e91313`

Background: The development of structured optical character recognition (OCR) systems for Korean medical documents is hindered by strict privacy regulations that limit access to real clinical data. Existing synthetic document frameworks are primarily designed for generic layouts and Western languages, and fail to capture the complex formatting, bilingual terminology, and hierarchical field semantics required for clinical OCR and document-understanding tasks in hospital environments. Objective: This study aimed to develop and validate a privacy-preserving, template-driven pipeline for generating realistic synthetic Korean medical documents with structured XML annotations, enabling OCR and document-understanding model training in settings where access to real clinical data is restricted. Methods: We designed MedDocSynth, a 7-stage synthetic document generation pipeline that reconstructs authentic medical document layouts from publicly available references, incorporates clinician-guided template selection, and uses a structured placeholder system encoding 17 medical data types. Human-curated templates were populated with exemplar content and tagged using a delimiter-based scheme, after which GPT-4o-mini generated diverse synthetic values for each field. The pipeline produced 1000 fully synthetic DOCX documents with corresponding XML annotations encoding hierarchical structure, title-answer relationships, and datatype metadata. We evaluated text fidelity, lexical diversity (type-token ratio, measure of textual lexical diversity [MTLD], n-grams, and Pareto), and compared the system with existing synthetic document pipelines. A Qwen2.5-VL model was fine-tuned exclusively on the synthetic dataset and evaluated on 50 real-world Korean clinical documents to assess OCR accuracy, information extraction, and table reconstruction. Results: The pipeline achieved 100% placeholder coverage, 99.68% format correctness, and 91.56% semantic accuracy. Lexical diversity analysis demonstrated high variability in free-text fields (MTLD≈456) and expected repetitiveness in structured datatypes. Compared with existing pipelines (SynthText, SynthTiger, and DocSynth), the proposed system uniquely supports complex clinical forms, bilingual content, and structured XML annotations. When trained solely on the synthetic data, Qwen2.5-VL achieved the highest real-world performance, with a character accuracy of 86.01%, word accuracy of 78.25%, field accuracy of 67.18%, datatype accuracy of 64.14%, and table reconstruction tree-edit-distance similarity score of 71.09%. Models without fine-tuning performed substantially worse, including GPT-5-mini. Conclusions: We present the first synthetic data generation pipeline specifically tailored to Korean medical documents, enabling the creation of realistic, richly annotated datasets for structured OCR without exposing patient information. The synthetic data significantly improves OCR performance on real clinical documents, demonstrating the pipeline’s potential utility for hospital deployment and its potential extensibility to other languages and health care systems.

### [The Role of Measurement in Identifying High-Intensity Secure Message Senders Among Older Adults: Observational Study](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e93641)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `55` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e93641`

Background: Use of secure messaging within the patient portal has grown rapidly over recent years, yet clinical workflows, staffing models, and billing practices have generally not been comprehensively adapted to support this increasing volume of messages. Little is known about the characteristics of patients who are high-volume message senders, and we lack a nuanced understanding of patient messaging intensity beyond measures accounting for sheer volume, limiting health system efforts to anticipate message-related workflow demands and allocate resources effectively. Objective: Our objective was to characterize older adult patients (aged ≥65 years) with high secure messaging volume, examining both patient characteristics and other aspects of their messaging intensity, such as messaging frequency, length, and messaging use relative to patient portal logins and health care encounters. Methods: We analyzed electronic medical record and patient portal data from a large academic health system, encompassing 16,023 older adults who sent 199,981 messages over a 12-month period. We developed 5 measures to account for secure messaging intensity. Our primary measure of messaging intensity was based on message volume; high-volume message senders were identified using outlier analysis based on patients’ aggregate number of messages sent during the observation period. Additional measures of messaging intensity included identifying individuals with concentrated periods of messaging, message length (character count), a ratio of messages to portal logins, and a ratio of messages to health care encounters. We compared sociodemographic characteristics, health status, and messaging intensity of high-volume secure messaging senders with those of other message senders. Results: Of 16,023 older adult patients who sent at least 1 message during the observation period, 1298 (8.1%) were classified as high-volume message senders; these patients accounted for 39.7% (79,475/199,981) of total messages. High-volume message senders, compared with all other message senders, were more likely to be White (1044/1298, 80.4% vs 10,670/14,725, 72.5%; &lt;.001), have higher comorbidity scores (2.6 vs 1.8; &lt;.001), and higher incidence of cancer (465/1298, 35.8% vs 3354/14,725, 22.8%; &lt;.001) and dementia (107/1298, 8.2% vs 889/14,725, 6.1%; &lt;.002). High-volume message senders were also more likely to be identified as having concentrated periods of messaging, to send longer messages, and to send more messages in relation to patient portal logins and health care encounters. Conclusions: High-volume message senders represent a small but distinct group of older patients who send a large share of messages to clinicians. Using multiple measures of messaging intensity can provide additional context about patient messaging behavior and help health systems redesign workflows and care delivery models to meet patient communication needs while facilitating sustainable workloads for clinicians. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/8359ac9ca22692bfe90126cc09175e0c" />

### [Towards reliable morphological subtyping and cancer detection on Barrett’s esophagus pathology through ensembling foundation models](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03329-3)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `61` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03329-3`

The reliability of emergent histopathology foundation models (Histo-FMs) for real-world diagnostic tasks remains unproven. Given the complexity of diagnostic tasks and the challenges inherent in real-world data, we utilized Histo-FMs to investigate their utility for morphological subtyping of Barrett’s esophagus (BE) and detecting esophageal adenocarcinoma (EAC) across the BE progression spectrum. We benchmarked Histo-FMs on the BarrettNet cohort, spanning diagnostic categories ranging from normal to EAC (N2EAC). The dataset comprised 3528 hematoxylin and eosin (H&amp;E)-stained whole-slide images (WSIs) from 790 patients. A multi-magnification, multi-backbone aggregation of the five most expert-consistent single-scale models yielded incremental performance gains (AUROC of 0.907, F1-score of 0.696, accuracy of 0.795, and κ of 0.651 for morphological subtyping; AUROC of 0.909, F1-score of 0.836, accuracy of 0.959, and κ of 0.673 for EAC detection), with non-uniform gains across single-model comparisons and limited benefit for dysplasia. The findings support the capability of Histo-FMs for BE morphological subtyping and EAC detection in a multicenter clinical cohort, while highlighting persistent challenges in dysplasia assessment and the need for external validation. This study provides a foundation for future clinical validation.
